A WARRINGTON PROVISION DEALER AND HIS RENT,

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before His Honour Judge Wynn?. Ffoulkes' Henry Thornton, the Laurels, Padgate, sued Patrick Sexton, provision dealer, of Hall-street, for £2 23. 6.1., a quarter's rent for a stable in Rose and Crown-street. Mr. Forshaw ap- peared for the plaintiff, and the defendant was repre- sented by Mr. Browne.—Mr. Forshaw said the rent was due on the fith of AuguA. Defendant hal been tenant of the stables for some years. Plaintiff had applied for the rent, but no notice had been taken of the application by defendant.—Henry Thornton said he owned the stables, and defendant was his tenant.

He had been tenant since 1893, and had paid for the stables quarterly. A qoarter's rent, £2 23 61, was now due.—By Mr. Br-wns ; He did not remember Mr. James Seaton. defendant's brother, paying a quarter's rent in May, 1893. He (plaintiff) did not enter the payment of the rent and then say that be had not got a receipt stamp. He did not say he would take the book and re- turn it with the payment receipted. He took the hook back. Mr. Sexton had not called on him to ask him for the b )ok. He did not remember anyone calling for it. Mr. Seaton had named the matter to hm. Hi (plaintiff's) son had not told him that the quarter's rent at that time was paid to him under protest. Mr. Sexton had not repeatedly told him that be was paying under protest. He had spoken to him several times about the book. He did not know what he wanted it for.— James Sexton, brother of the defendant, said that on the 6th of May, 1894, Mr. Thcruton called at his shop, and he handed him toe rent bcok and £2 2i 6d. He entered something, and then said he had no stamp. He asked if he might be allowed to take the book to his effice, and he would send it back receipts d. Witness allowed him to do so, but had never since seen the book. When Mr. Thornton was away his son called and de- manded the rent. Witness tcld him it had been paid to his father. Be (the son) threatened to distrain, and witneis paid him the rent under protest. He saw Mr. Thornton when he came home, and he searched his , offiae for the book. He asked witness to leave the matter over for the pres eat, as he was is a hurry. Witness had sent to him many times since. On every occasion when he had pail him during the last flys, years he had mentioned the matter, and asked him each rime for the book.—Patr:ck S.mton, the last wit- ness's son, and Thomas Mullin said they went for the rent book four or five years ago.—His Honour said he was afraid he could not consider that a satisfactory proof of payment. He could not ur3der:tand a man ccn• tinning to pay rent without settling ve,th regard to the fir,t payment. There was nothing on the subsequent rscripts to show that they had been paid under protest. There would be judgment for the plaintiff.

E EXAMINER MUD 'k 17, NOVEMBER 25 1898

leave this lovely place—as indeed you were only a .i-re. he winced, and I added: "Be Wi3o, and adapt yourself to circumstances. If men and women were moulded to your pattern, they would not long please you, unless you were contented with your own lot." "True," she responded thoughtfully. "The rest- less steed needs bit and bridle." t "I must apologise for speaking plainly," I added, RS Lucia began to pout and fidget with her fan, "but you swore to assist us in tracking the robbers at Puke's Delight, and the ringleaders of the attack on San Leno. What have you done? Nothing! I begin to doubt your sincerity." She stcuortneehdrnwith void flashingof s eenyteismuepnot,n understandm e.'c "flow can aa Castilian!" Then she paced to and fro, remind- ing me of an angry puma in a menagerie cage. "Tell me," she cried, "wherein have I deceived :ou ? If Mark desires the marauders' punishment, why remain in Golden Vale fooling his time away with this offspring of af dusky woman ? " O. Lucia's sarcastic tone was so bitter that it made my flesh creep, so I observed, with considerable asperity: "Perhaps Mark is using his own methods to detect the parties. If he tracks them, woe be to those who shield them." "Doubtless you are in his confidence," she replied, "while I am ignored." t "That is neither kind to me, Senorita, " I retorted warmly, "nor just to my friend. His object in coming here is, probably, no mystery to you or me. I am here because, since he pays the wages, he has a right to my loyalty." Here the complex character of the Castilian chewed itself, for Lucia laughed heartily—just that merry ripple of laughter which is contagious. She plucked a flower, and pulled out the petals, saying: "And is that all he came for? " My face wore a dubious look as I shrugged my shoulders and rendered into the best Spanish at my command: "Senorita, your pardon. lam a blockhead." I bowed with unusual grace, for I own to not being very cavalierly, and the tone of my voice was sarcastic. Lucia's unfeigned amusement convinced• me that she construed my words as a huge joke. But there, I never ccurrd fathom a woman, so, in utter despair, I gave up trying. My embarrassment was rendered somewhat galling, because Mark at that moment entered the alcove, and said playfully: "I apologise for my unseemly intrusion." While my neck and face got hot Lucia rose to the occasion. Rushing forward she threw her arms around his neck, and in a most winsome way, said: "Mark, I have been wondering whether Olive has won your love from me, and in my usual impulsive way I have expressed my fears to your best friend." And, turning towards me, her eyes apparently sparkling with admiration and her lovely face beaming with gladness, she daintily added: "Believe me, Mark, he is your best friend." "Don't run away, Sandy.," said my chief good- humouredly. "/ am not Jealous." "Jealous o' me!" I retorted, knitting my brows and tapping my breast, "how absurd!" Mark nodded and smiled—one of those sugges- tive smiles that makes a man wish- he could throw something at the wearer. The situation was awkward, nevertheless. Mark affectionately kissed the Senorita, and told her not to be jealous without good and sufficient reason. In his crisp and kindly way he denied in- constancy, adding: "Queen Rana wishes the friendly relations of our party to be cemented by investing me with the order tat the Squirrel—a knighthood shewing the personal -favor of the Queen, and held in as high esteem as tht British Garter." "Rana is a'ready baiting a hook," I wl...quered. "She expects to haul a big fish." Mark gave a short, vexed sigh. , Lucia muttered something sotto voce, the y urport of which he comprehended, for he ejaculated: "Fiddlesticks!" She raised her eyebrows and asked quietly: "May I see the ceremony ? " "I'm afraid not," he replied, "but I'll ask it as a favour, and if liana agrees I'll send for you. But, Lucia, whether you come or not, you need have no fear about Olive 'winning my love." And turning to me, he added, in English: "If Rana is fishing with the proclivities of a match-making mamma, I'll come the fox-like artfulness of ,a grey mullet—dart to the side of the Queen's net and leap the head-rope into the open sea." ' "But the net inebbe raised too high," said I, "an' mak' this impracticable." "Then I'll creep under it," he responded with an amused smile. "What if that mode o'escape be coot aff " "Then I will search for a defective mesh." "Ala," I reflected, "if as daring as a mullet, yet ye're na sufficiently ingenious." "Believe me, Lucia," he added, in Spanish, placing his arm fondly about her waist as he looked wistfully upon her pleading face, "I'm not susceptible to the influence of a half-caste, even though she were a Venus." "Perhaps not, Mark," answered the Spanish lady, her face now wreathed in smiles, "but you are ignorant of the kind of decoys used to bring in the wild ducks. However, I'll trust you, but take care and not mention Ituro's name, or these semi- barbarians will chop you into rniAce-meat." Mark laughed, and kissed her hand lightly. Then he set out for the palace, and we went home.

CHAPTER XV. TUE GREEN-EYED MONSTER.

Now one evening Olive strolled about the out- skirts of our colony listening to the music and merriment. Mark caught sight of her, and in his frank style went to her, and with signs and smiles welcomed the Princess to our circle. In coaxing her to this end, Lucia thought he was longer than necessary, for she tripped among the luxuriant flowers and jealously watched the couple. At last my good friend's gentle persuasion pre- vailed on the half-caste, who was ushered to the seat of honour. This piqued Lucia, and the first pangs of jealousy began to tear asunder the bonds of affection between her and the Princess. She suddenly grew reserved, and pleaded indisposition when called on for a song and dance. She recom- mended Olive's musical genius to us, and gave such a glowing account of her grace and style that Mark was forced to believe her sarcasm the outcome ot wounded pride. Yet how he had contributed to this did not dawn upon him, and so he entered more heartily into Olive's merriment. This annoyed Lucia, who retaliated by playing the coquette with me. Finding I tolerated none such silliness, she soon excused herself, pleading that a bad headache drove her home.

But when Mark met her later on, they were observed to walk together just in that stiff manner known to buffed lovers, and afterwards I saw by his troubled face that Lucia had given him a good talking to. Furthermore, when I spoke to him he snapped me off sharply. Like the poet Bunthorne, I could only sigh and mutter: "Crushed again!" Now a week or so after, we were at the palace. and the Senorita came tripping towards Mark and me, her eyes sparkling with some new fascination and her pretty face flushed with excitement. "Come," she said warmly, "I have mounted the tower and beheld a paradise around. It is a picture, the like no mortal outside Golden Vale has seen. There is not -a cloudlet in the sky, and the gentle breeze tempers the sunglow ; sweet are the bird-songs, and infinite the charm of the scenery!" And after a flow of praise in idiomatic Spanish, Lucia wound up with: "To live amid such glory te Olive's happy lot; how I envy her! Mark," she added sotto voce, as if a thought suddenly occurred to her, "Pecos tells me it is the wish of this fascinating girl that you should go and see the beautiful view from the tower, where she awaits you.,,

Lucia's winsome smile set Mark wondering, and he significantly observed to me, "Sandy, I've been with Pecos to get a shave, and picked up some news. A barber's shop is always the hotbed of gossip. I'm afraid liana's treasure-house is im- pregnable. This I know, that if Muro prospers, or gets access to Golden Vale, some she-devil in this valley helps him." "Hush, Mark!" I rebuked in an undertone. "reople may think ye refer to liana, if they catch half a word."

"Come, Lucia," said he aloud, "let us go up the tower." But the Senorita was gazing out of the window humming a Spanish melody to the pianissimo accompaniment of her guitar. I sighed, for a presentiment of trouble burdened my soul. "Pardon me," said I persuasively to Mark, "dinna be swaytd by hearsay. I think we're per- fectly safe free asslssination here—if the Queen's person remains nviolable "

"Well," he mumbled severely, his face sternly set, "Kane hangs her traitors in chains at the &ate of the city, as food fit only for vultures. The man or woman who attempts to betray me sLall receive a like fate."

He proudly mounted the winding stone stairs into the watch tower, where Olive's smile of welcome soon thawed his icy manner—so rare, and yet in this instance not altogether unaccountable, from the events of the last few days. I passed over to where Lucia Diaz was humming, and tapped her shoulder. She half turned with surprise, her face crestfallen. Fortunately, none were near to witness this scene. "Senorita," I observed kindly in Spanish, "take warning, and beware of harbouring evil thoughts. Misery is the handmaid of Jealousy!" "You think my character transparent," she answered lightly, turning her head from me. "I weigh you as though you were a jockey about to mount against one rival." She laughed and tossed her head, saying: "Olive's horse is scratched,' and I have wont A man likes a coquette just so much as she excites the envy of his male acquaintances. If she lingers in their company when he whistles for her, then he fancies his exclusive rights are attacked; he grinds his teeth, clenches his fists, swears, and gets drunk, as a kind of retaliation—thus debasing his body and demoralising his mind. if she does not sing cheerf,kly and coax away his despondency, then he tells her she is getting old and crabbed, She may have aches and pains—the effects of which must be confined to her boudoir. If she feels depn seed, her face must still wear a serene smile." "Senorita, I protest—" "Nay, hear me out! She won by blandishments —to continue her reign, mockery and deception must be fashionably maintained to the end of the chapter. In his own estimation the man alone fights life's battles; the woman's beauty must per- petually charm, her grace fascinate, her accom- plishments win favour with recognised critics. The unpleasant s d 3 of the picture is when, like a tarnished t y, she is cast as de lest the other fellows sh uld s'e that her skin begins to wrinkle. . . . Is tli s the rigs Mark Pepys expects me to play? Fan h!" She si apped her fingers, laughed derisively, and then when i again dep ored her suspi, Lug nature, the hot tears ro led dcwn her flushed , he ,ks as she retorted: "I Castil al jealous of a half-caste Indian! Bahl you do not know Lucia Diaz!" "So it appears," said I frankly, as I saw the fierce glitter of her beautiful eyes. Thereupon, with head erect and step firm, she walked away to a quiet nook, there to cry and sob as if her heart would break. The chameleon never changed its colour more quickly than Lucia changed her temper —women were always a puzzle to me, but this one was incomprehensible. Mischief was brewing already'. -If Olive was to eclipse Mark's love, what sort of a shadow would it throw on Lucia's life? The Senorita had stolen to my side. She tugged my arm wildly, and I wheeled round, looking intently at her tear-dimmed eyes and flushed face. "Sefior Dees," she observed, spitefully, in a hushed voice. "I know the secret of Mark Pepys's visit to Golden Vale. If Kane had acquired some of his more valuable treasures, he could not kidnap the mother of the Princess whom he saved, and who has won his love. If he attempts to use Olive as an instrument to effect his purpose, he may find, when he breaks into the royal treasure-house, that he is caught like a rat in a trap. Take my advice: Keep your own counsel. Let him lose his honour and throw his love to the dogs, if he likes; but save your own skin!" She disappeared without another word, some mad resolve agitating her mind. Her warning enabled me to take time by the forelock. If this new phase did not make Mark heartily ashamed of his treachery, then I would insist on his abandoning his dangerous adventure before we were bowled over.

Now we had gleaned many interesting facts from Olive when a Queen's messenger summoned us.into the royal presence. Mark and the Princess led the way, but as I lingered a little, Lucia came and touched my arm lightly with her fan. I tailed, and gathered from her gestures that she wished a word with me, so I walked aside with her into an alcove.

"I am weary of Golden Vale," she said pensively. "How long before we depart ? " "Mark Pepys is master," I replied coldly. "To hear is to obey." "Your manner freezes me," she retorted, nervously tapping her palm with her closed fan. She sighed, and her handsome lips quivered. Thep she lowered her arched eyebrows, zna I saw mingled flame and me," in her eyes. "Your reticence tortures me, she added; "I would rather my heart's love were killed outright than suffer the misery of suspense. These courageous and fearless men often make fools of themselves when my sex fascinates them. History always repeats itself: Mark is a full-blooded Saxon—a type of the old Vikings—and remember human nature is to-day what it was centuries ago. Candidly, I fear the influence of Olive. Let us go hence—and I beseech you, Seilor Dees, advise Mark to this end, and that quickly. . . . Ahl you think me selfish in this request!" _"No " I exclaimed testa, "bat I out loth to

CHAPTER XVL

TRZ RITALEI. As a sense of oppression weighed on me I threw• myself on the couch and dreamt of Lucia Diaz, and woke with a splitting headacraa. Then drowsily turning on any side, again I dreamt of her; and yet a third time, until the gloaming was far spent and the moon's light began to tip the crags and play upon the smoke that issued from the volcanic cone to the south of the city. Suddenly a commotion in the next apartment startled me, and even while I rubbed my eyes and drew my hand across my aching forehead Mark came in full of excitement, his face flushed and his brow knit with anger. And yet, with it all, there was the high-bred look of the refined gentle- man, the clever bridling of passion that marked the patrician born—the aristocratic profile, not so much handsome as thoroughbred and noble. "What has ruffled yer temper, Mark ? " "Lucia annoyed me, and we quarrelled! Slat twitted me in a contemptuous way about Olive!" "I dislike a nagging wuman," said I unguardedly. "I don't.know that a jealous one is much better," he responded, with a little more serenity of manner. "Lucia has evidently been chafing sorely." "A. wuman's mind is like a patchwork quilt, made up o' varied moods, frae the slighting languor o' ennui to the sardonic phase o' tragic jealousy. I suppose the Sefiorita goaded ye to fury? " 'Yes, and I retaliated in a brutal fashion, for I accused her of being in league with Muro." I sighed, feeling that Mark ,had been foolish. "An' her reply ? " "She flatly denied it. But she could not, or, at least, refused to deny that in Yerba Buena she had been on very friendly terms with Muro. I've been gathering evidence bit by bit, and dovetailing trivial words and actions until the truth is almost revealed."

"Na, na, trark," I replied, shaking my head seriously.- "In the hidden meesteries o' human nature ye're no' i' the running e'en wi' Kana's philosophers. Ye hae made a stupid blunder— excuse my bluntness." "Well, it is galling to be told so," he answered, sitting down, "but it is straight." "Beware, lest this design turn yer upper garret awry. Yer sudden fit o' impatience has saddled us wi' a jealous wuman. There's an use o' crying o'er spilt meelk, but remember I warned ye at the start."

"What is your advice ? " he asked in a tone of fatigue. "Accept her denial guid grace; gang slang as usual, an' if there's further cause fer the least sus- picion send her back to San Leno. That's my advice—tak' it or leave it." - -

A soft step and gentle sobbing in the corridor warned us to listen. Soon the supplicating •Lucia had glided in and thrown herself at Mark's feet. In Spanish she plaintively appealed for forgive- ness, if her past friendship *ith Muro had vexed him; and anon she arrested my attention with a trite cry for 'justice. "Did I not forsake the gaiety and pleasures of Yerba Buena. to be by your side, Mark? Did I not slay the Indian guidit upon the tower of San Leno, when your lives were weighing in the balance? Who helped defend the Presidio ?—I. Who nursed the sick ?—I. You know that over the San Leno graves I vowed to revenge the death of your brave men. No woman, guilty of treachery, would do that. The delay in tracking the culprits is not of my seeking' for here, in Golden Yale, rev bands are tied. I base solemnly

sworn to avenge the San Leno siege." And in idiomatic Spanish she added: "Notwithstanding all this, yet you rake up the past and chew mg weeds that I thought were buried for ever. Aftel having praised my wine you give me vinegar. Mark, I beseech you, have pity on me. Your frown wounds my heart; the loss of your love would be death to me!"

The Beiiorita's grief was most distressing ; indeed, I, designated a woman-hater, was upset at seeing her tears. Now what were Mark and I that we should throw mud at this woman? As she clung to him and buried her burning head on his breast he made a grimace for assistance out of the dilemma. Any observer with half an eye could see he was passionately fond of her, though ashamed to shew his weakness. I took pity on him, but expressed my displeasure by grunting out: "Those wha mix their am n medicine mustna complain if it's bitter."

"Dry your eyes, Lucia," he said, in a far kinder way than I could have commanded under the circumstances. "When relations are strained, a storm sometimes clears the air. Come back all I said. You shewed your hasty temper, and I turned like a bulldog. We shall now understand each other better. It is my earnest wish that you should enjoy your visit to Golden Vale. You were silly to be jealous of Olive. Queen Kana means well by me "—with a sigh—"immeasurably beyond nity deserts; and shortly she starts a round of fetes en our honour. Now, throw yourself heart and soul into the pleasure." If Olive were to win your love, Mark," she replied with evident sincerity, "there would be nothing in life worth living for." He kissed her affectionately, glad in his heart for the opportunity of making up the quarrel—for Mark had a good disposition, and was a manly, noble fellow.

- Caressing her till she bvightened into her normal condition, he advised her to go to bed early; and when she left us with that intention, he asked me his best course to regain her confidence and to remove from her mind the impression that he was in love with the Princess. "There's an auld Spanish proverb," said I, "which fits yer case: • There's na lock but a golden key will open it.' " "I tumble," answered Mark. "dome along, Sandy, let us go to the best jewellers shop in Golden Vale." -

Together we went, and Mark bartered for a lovely gold necklace as a present for Lucia. Now, a testy man will soften after a square meal, but a woman is better pleased with a rich gift for personal adornment. So when we returned, and Mark gave Lucia the present, her delight knew no bounds. After, thanking him, and assuring him of her con- stancy, tears of joy filled her eyes. This golden gift was a token of reconciliation, and the ardent, Mark Pepys again assured her that he regretted having lost his temper through harbouring silly suspicions. 'lt's the auld, auld story, Mark; the best fellow i' the world who is a'ways at hame is apt to become a bore. After this estrangement ye'll saddle horses richt weel."

"Repeated tiffs destroy confidence on both sides" said he thoughtfully. "This disagreement is entirely my fault. I was far too hasty.:

"Then to-morrow tak' the lass to -the theatre" was my response; "an' if liana has designs, lat her see that Olive canna cut Lucia oot."

"What!" he exclaimed, half-ridiculing the idea, "do you think for one moment that a dusky Queen can pit a half-caste Princess against my Lucia? Ridiculous!" When, therefore, on the morrow Mark took the Senorita to the show, her clear complexion, grace, and charms—enhanced by Mark's present—won the admiration not only of Olive, but of the Queen herself. This flattered Lucia, and so the time went right merrily. A rich present will work wonders with a vain woman, especially if she thinks it excites the envy of her rival. After the performance, and prior to breaking up, sweet juices were banded round, and the Court Master of Ceremonies cried aloud, in his vernacular that Kana wished all to till their glasses for a toast. Then her Majesty rose, saying with a melodious voice, as she smiled at Mark: ..‘2l vuestra salud "

"To your health!" reiterated the High Priest in the native tongue; whereupon the Compliment was repeated by all, and Kana lifted the glass to her lips. From where I stead I saw that the Queen heel- tat el as if paralysed, and consternation was written on every face. Suddenly the air was filled with smoke, and a sulphurous smell caught one's breath. Then beneath our feet distinct rumblings were heard, and within the time occupied by a squirrel's cry the earth trembled and shook. Frightened out of their wits, some of the more excitable females had hysterical fits, and Mark did his level best to calm Lucia's fears. Olive threw her arras about her mother's neck, but the Queen stood with stoics indifference, the while many ran into the open for safety. Presently the earth-quivering ceased, the •lpen-glow was less obscure, and we had time to cs...atily look round. Kana's self-command saved a pauic, but the theatre soon cleared. Upon making inquiries we learnt that`the oldest inhabitants remembered such a shock in their youth, when some changes occurred in the configuration of the valley. Happily, beyond a severe strain, the city sustained no great damage on this occasion, but several fissures in the sides of the voloir indicated the scat of the disturbance. "W tkta days and nights the cone was agitated, week even that subsided; and soon dultsrgte quillity of Golden Vale permitted a --;4isistropsse the amphitheatre in our 'honour. Prizes in coin and rich kind were offered, and our people entered heartily into the contests. In the shooting competition our men won by long chalks, but for a chain of gold offered by the fair Olive for the feat of greatest strength none would compete with Goya. Mark watched this man keenly from within the royal enclosure. Observing the mighty man's vanity, I slid up to my friend and whispered in his ear: "Mark, here's a chance to 'establish yourself i' the guid books o' the sports- men. That big fellow looks as strong as a bear, but he's clumsy," "I'm not in first-rate trim, Sandy." "Nonsense. Yer daily life is a guid training.* "Besides, I don't covet the honour." "Perhaps no'," I urged, "but yer success would be another string t' yer bow." I selfishly thought that if danger threatened us, Mark's popularity might save us. Oh, unlucky persuasion! How I afterwards hated myself for suggesting the match! "If Olive placed that chain round my neck, Lucia might sorely chafe." "Weel," I responded, "if the Seilorita becomes jealous again we may learn something. Remember, we has t' find oot the part Puke played; an' there's an auld score to settle wi' Muro, whose name nobody dares mention here on pain o' instant death. This decree was made years ago." Mark bit his nether lip and cast my words about in his mind. "Sandy, go at once to Lucia; tell her I think of challenging this strong man. If she agrees and I am beaten, she will have the good sense to accept my defeat with grace." "An' harbour less pique against the Princess," I mentally- observed while proceeding to carry out his mission. I put it to Lucia as if the suggestion came from me, and the pretty bird was caught in the harm- less snare. "Though Mark fail," she observed, "his 3hallenge, where none of the tribe dare even pre- tend, will Win him more popularity. Tell him it is my wish that he measure his strength with this Indian." .

- Negotiations ended, my stalwart friend strolled into the arena amid cries of surprise and some jeers. The champion was boastful, and eyed his white opponent with disdain. If •it looked like swagger, when Mark threw down his hat and tucked up his sleeves in true British fashion, people saw he meant business. The challenger smiled with derision, then saluted, after which Mark shook hands in a friendly way. In the first tug-of-war knack worsted my chief, but not without a struggle. In the second tug he used similar means, and pulled the Indian across the line. In the third both pulled fair and Nuare, and the time-kepeer gave it as a draw. 7hus, in this, Mark was the Indian's equal. In throwing wrought-iron balls the dusky skin came off triumphant, but he lost in slinging heavier weights, and one could tell by the excite- ment among all classes, that had betting prevailed the bookmakers would have been bewildered. But gambling was forsworn. I noticed the mortification of the tribe's favourite, because many shouted to. him words of cheer; but his agitation was more noticeable when he beheld Mark beckoning him to further try his skill. This he did with some misgiving, for while Mark sat down and smilingly rubbed his calves, an attepdant bound a horsehair cord about the Inelan's wrists, and rubbed his muscles with a lotion. Evidently the bouts had already strained . _

He advanced for Olive to bestow her. smiles and hand him the prize. His giant strength was the crowning point in the eyes of the Golden Vale ople, which transformed him into a demi-god. They were noted for hero-worship, and my only fear was that my friend might get spoilt—for honours may transform a man into an animated body of self-conceit. However, he had the good sense to make his profound obeisance to Kana— though the effort cost him much heart-burning—and after receiving her congratulations, he pleased Lucia by handing her the prize. in. Sefiorita's eyes sparkled; but I noticed Olive's languishing look, and the soft, beautiful eyes of her Queen- mother comprehended more than the lips could utter.

The oracle was beginning to work. (To be continued.)

LADLES' LETTER.

Illy DaAu Nzu,no.

The colour most in vogue in the world of fashion prove to be unusually light for the winter, bright reds, vivid violets, that rich shade of Magenta known as jaoquemot, and blues of a deep, full tone being popular. They are all PO much trimmed with black as greatly to modify their brightness. There is quite an absurd run upon what is called " true-lovers' kitots," which the French know as "Louis XV. knots." They are

placed upon innumerable hats, and form the soya design of endless embroideries. They are cut otit of taffeta or velvet, and applied on other materiall, or they are twisted in the course of applications of lines and rows of braid or of velvet ribbon. In satin ribbon, too, they are effectively made by first drawing the ribbon at each edge to form a not very thick ruche, and then disposing it in the knob shape over a layer of cotton wool, so that it stands up in relief; this should not be done with velvet., as it has a heavy appearance. *

Millinery when it is at its best matches the dresses. A pretty silk or velFet hat can be Simply trimmed with a couple of rosettes, centred by a glass button, from which springs one of the new feathers composed of very thick osprey gemmed with little pheasants' eyes. Velvet is to be quite a leading feature of millinery. Hats are either trimmed with it or at entirely composed of velvet, trimmed with feathers Cr quills. There is a craze for violets in the millinery of to-day. Fur toques are fashionable trimmed on one side with violets or holly and leaves as fancy. dictates. Fashions in London are pretty much the same, at the present moment, as they are in Paris. Ihere is a lavish display of trimmings of every kind—ruchings, fringes, chenille spotted net, sequined net, ribbons, narrow velvet, with Empire bows, lace, and fur. Fur capes, mantles, jackets, sollarsi tippets, and fur muffs are in abundance everywhere. Chinchilla is much favoured. There is a tempting little shoulder #oollarette of fur, finished off at the throat with lace, and a lace bow, which promises to become more popular even that the fur boa, with which all are familiar. High nollars of fur are worn lined with silk or satin and lace. Many of them are frilled. These have rather a clumsy appearance.

Fur Jackets are very fashionable. They are lined with silk and cut in the sacque shape at the back. They are particularly becoming in the douhle•breaated style with large revere.

Grey inni-lins . and ntousselines de soie still hold their own on dry days, and look veiy nice unless a Sudden shower comes on, and then their glory departs swiftly. No foundation is really suitable except grey silk or silkettee if the dainty dove-like tint is to be preserved. A very pretty one seen recently owes some of its summer siii ability to the fact that it was not burdened w, ' flounces or _frills, but the round skirt %vs. '.5 white Valenciennes (imitation) Nee - :tore than an inch wide, above which , .4 of amber 'me ribbon, then a row o; on, followed ty four rows of the arDl)4r ..01 so on and so an till only two rows we, ,i above the last frisertion. The sleeves ' bad small grey puffs, but between wrist and elbow 4). piece ot the sash ribbon was laid- round with !admirable effect, and finished with lace frills that looked very delicate over pale grey suede four- :button gloves. The grey silk muslins are chiefly adorned with black satin or gauze ribbon, the latter looking far more in keeping with so diap'nanous a fabric and very light black blonde :,.!ae: in Chantilly designs. . 4 ' A very stylish gown, which is suitable either for Walking or skating, is of cinnamon-brown faced :cloth, braided in a scroll design with fancy mohair and gold tinsel, and the collar, revers, and cuffs .ftre of the choicest mink tail fur. " WOMEN'S CHAT."

On Monday last there was a large gathering of the Royal Family at Windsor Castle, and in the evening tbe Queen gave a dinner party to celebrate the birthday of the Empress Frederick—our Princess Royal. The dining room, in which the banquet was served, ia only used by the Queen when there are more than eighteen persens at table. As a rule, her Majesty dines and lunches in the Oak Room, which opens Irene the corridor, and over- looks the quadranele: A prominent object in the dining room is the large picture with equestrian portraits of the Prince of Wales, and the Duke of Connaught. It occupies a space on the West well, just above the immense sideboard,which is of rosewood, and superbly carved. Another notable fea- ture in this room is the sumptuous punch bowl with the ladle to match, which was made by Hendon and Budge for George IV. at the cast of 10,000 guineas, the bill being paid by the country. No soccer does the Queen reture from her autumn sojourn in the Flighlands,than the business of Christmas present choesing commences in real earnest. Jae now the Sovereign's private apartments are a vesitable bazaar, and the process ot selection is going mere de on. To secure just the thing which will suit each individual taste Is the great ercleaveur of the Queen, and needless to say she is invariably successful in ail, particular direction. Toys for the little ones are always well to the fore, and, it has been whispered that Her Majesty en- joys this part of the selection • above all others. Te the ladies of her household the Queen usually gives Christ- mas presents of jewellery. The Court will leave Windsor on Tuesday December 20h, for Osborne and Her Majesty is to residein te 131 e of Wight for about eight weeks. There is a sarnenese stateiy and austere about the preparations for Chi i es at Osborne. Thebaby grand- children who danced round the splendid Christmas tree a decade back, have now become demure little maids in the schoolroom, and sturdy boys in the Eton playing. fields, but other babies have taken their places,and none but their fathers and mothers, should they suddenly look in on the Christmas scene, would be aware that any chanee of persons had occurred. As with the Royal in- mates, so it is with the decoration of chapel and saloon, the gifts to kinsfolk,and to loyal rctainers,the do`e to the peer at the gates, and the menu for the table in the In- dian room, which farms the splendid banqueting hell of the day. An amusement of visitors at Osborne, the week before the 25th, is to go into the kitchens, and survey the Qrwen's plum puddings. Of these the number appears countless, for besides those made for the high table, the nurserics,the household, the servants' haleand the poor, there are rows of the great brown globes, destined as I presents for Her Majesty's relatives abroad. It is well- I known that no authentic plum pudding can be manu- factured beyond the boundaries of the British Nee, and recognising tbis fact, the Queen supplies those served on Christmas Day, en the Imperial tables of St. Petersburg, and Berlin.and on other boards round which her descen- dents gather at this season. It seems only yesterday that the engagement of the Duke of Marlteroveh to Miss Consuelo Vanderbult was first announced. In fact, it is a little more than three years since the much talked of marriage was celebrated, and the baptism of their Grace's second son took place the other day in the Chapel Royal, St. Jamee's Palace. The Duke who is now in his twenty-eighth year, suc- ceeded to the title at twenty-one. He is pstron of nine livings, owner of Blenheim, and 23,000 acres. The Duchess is not pretty, but she has a good figure and is always very well dressed. Madame Patti's marriage with Baron Cedersieem will take place in the early spring, in Wales. The Baron, a young, handsome, neilitary-leeking man, made the acquaintance of the diva at Cannes last year. Towards the latter end of the past summer, he was invited to spend seine weeksat Craig-y-nos, and it was then the engaeement took place. In the ages of the bride and bridegroom elect there is some considerable discrepancy, and—all on the wrong side. Madame Patti has pasted her fifty-fifth year, the Baron is but eight and twenty. The honeymoon, according to present arrangements, will be spent in Sweden. Among women doctors in this country, one of the best known at the present time is Dr. Muriel Maitland- King. Notwithstanding the increased demands of h-r patients, she finds time to give most interesting lectures on various phases of nervous exhaustion, insomnia, embonpoint, etc.,and has written several medical works. She has three very handsome daughters, the youngest of the trio being married to Par. Jack Humphreys. Early rising has long been regarded as the royal road to health, wealth, and wisdom, but nowadays, many eminent doctors held the opinion that too much early rising is not good for anybody, more esp,ciany children. nem° little folks seem to require a good deal of sleep,and it is advisable, so say the men of medicine, to extend to these who are usually least willing to get up in the morning, any litt'e indulgence which does not un- reasonably interfere with the rentine of the day. One of the mos eenent specialists in the world on insanity bas lately added his testimony to the theory that early rising is a most prolific C 41190 of this disease. In support of this assertion he points out the higher percentage of lunacy in the country districtsrwhere most people are early risers. He says "The early morn- ing hours,when ev -rye} log is still, are peculiarly fitted for sleep, and it is a gross violation of all the laws of nature, to tear human brains out of the sound rest they enjoy at this time." Probably the vast majority find little difficulty' ha agreeing with this opinion. Sleep is like wealth, many of us could do with a little more, and others would be equally as well with perhaps, a little less. There is not much that carries conviction in the early rising lunacy theory, but there is no doubt whatever that a little ri - laxation of rigid rules is often beneficial to adults and children alike. Both in summer and winter, most women wear too many clothes. Such a mode of procedure interferee with the functions of the skin' and renders the wearers unhealthy. Fur is a sinner inthisrespect It is tbotieh so pretty, and so becoming a trimming that it is diffi- cult to resist its charms, and whatever censors may say, it will, in all prebability,continue to be worn—in r^7son and out of noise a. Furls much introduced on all the most up-to-date evening gowns, and a slender line of beaver, or of sable, mineling with the lace at -int the shoo'ders is an embellishment not to be equalled i e effect. We who are weary of the never endues 01'e - in women's dress, have reason to love for, lec 0-e no beads or sequins can possibly be sewn on it. Green is perhaps the favourite colour just new, but the preference for one particular tint is by no means so

marked as has been the case in former Seasons. The wonatn, however, who would look healthy should grea, le affect green, as its coot .ct at once deepens whateve. , the rose nature has bestowed on her. The rudel? health v, on the other hand, can take refuge in purple,or any of its kindred tones and grey, another cold colour, relieves itself with orange—but discreetly—for a little of it goes a long way. A hat I met, acconspanyiog a grey gown, at a reception receroly, was quite a sermota in the art el manceuvring tones. The shape was in a straw of brownish grev, the wings of white tipped with' brownish grey, aod dotted with dark sequins, and the wired coils of velvet a glowing orange. Those of us who take a pride in a well-.haprd foot, should patronise a laced hoot, and never a low walking 1 shoe of any kind. Buttoned boots are not to be recom- mended. They encourage the spreading cut, and nenstaral devoloprnent et a nicely-shaped foot. It matters not how firm the buttons, they" give" the Motions of the feet, and if ,weara regularly entirely ruin a pretty ankle. Laced boots on the (Ozer hand, tend greatly to the correction el any faults a foot may possess. Now is the time to put a piece cf beef in efte for Christmas. If a round, the butcher must be asked to oat one vety carefully, and of prime beef. The follew ing spices, etc, are necessary. llb Fait ; ill) brown F,J!zal- toz. of ground whits pepper,ioz.of ground alspice.some clover; and mace, put into the oven until dried, and then pounded up. Add half a te:apooirful of saltpetre, mix all the ingredients well, and rub the mixture into the beef. Put the meat in a tub, rub every day, turning daily for the first week, and afterwards every other day. The cooking requ'res care. .A 10Ih piece needs 4 hours simmering. The water must bit before the beef is put in, and about a pint of the brine from which it is taken should also be put in. This piced beet is excellent, either hot or cold. The fat is uncernmonly good, and is liked by persons who never touch ordinary fat.

WAItIIINGTON FIELD CLUB.

At the fourtti nteeting of the current session, Mr. E.E. Lowe, assistant at the Museum, delivered:a lecture, entitled " An introduction to the study of insects,' elucidating the subject with blackboard sketches, with some simple but most effective oriLinal diagrams, and with a number of carefully mounted dis:ections, prepared by the lecturer, and illustrating the comparative rnatonty of the princi- cal insect types. In a clear and well planned address, Mr. Lowe showed how a beginner might gradually acquire accurate knowledge oC the general characters and most striking peculiarities of the principal orders of insects, whilst at the same time retaining the enthusiasm of a collector for the ' special group at which he might be working. He touched briefly on the vexed question of clear:idea- tion, end rightly recognized that, at least for be- ginners it is better to be able to practically dis-, tinguish, to which of the half-dozen great orders one's captures are related then to confuse the mind at the outFet with the intermediate forms which connect the great groups oE insects with one an- other and with the more lowly ancestral types from which all have emerged. The Field Club is particularly strong on entomology • this fact, and the reputation Mr. Lowe has already wen, brought together a full attendance. There was a long and lively discussion, during which the lecturer was wartn‘y and deservedly c3nvatelated on h's per- formance.

BOLD STREET WESLEYAN CfIURCFI. On S inday at thie place of worship the Rev, H. G. Roberts M.A. preached is special sermon to young men by request of the Y.M.C.A.. There was an excellent congregation. Mr. Roberts took for his text " See if ye eaniust find a man." 5h chopter Jeremiah, Ist verse,and at the outeet describe I the state of Jerusalem. Vice walked her street:s. God had spoken many times through the centuries and roused up deliverers who by their heroisms and triumphs vindicated the divine choice in the Rational trust. Tneip was no doubt an element of pessimism in that expression. Ho denounced pessimism i and showed that t crippled the potential forces of the mind and soul. There was much pessimisms even in the present day. D ,rtebrowed prophets looked to the horizon and predicted stormy weather for our nation. As they looked at home and abr-i.d, there was much in kind. The unchailling East must pass thrllnh its revolution. Chino, the last of the barbaric sovereign- ties of the East mat pass through her revolution, sad notwithstanding the many fears and apprehensions they hoped the change!, of the. Etst and the tragical parable of Khartouen would receive their highest interpretation in the unfolding of Christian history. England's glory did not depend upon WPAIth or the energy or pluck of her sailors and soldiers but upon the righteousness of her policy, it was a good thing in this age of superficiality to be brought back to the stordy simplicities of life. It was a good thing to emspbasise that word—man. Then he referred to the caricatures that they had. One could net walk with his eyes open without stping strange types of juvenile manhood struttinit up and down the Sabbatical promenade of Warrington as if the eyes of Europe were fixed upon them and who found their pleasure on Sunday in contemptible levities and tomfooleries. Then the preacher referred to the popular standard of manhood —the physical. He would not say one word to dispar- age the games that braced the physical form, as after the labour of the day the working man required some source of recreation. There were public houses and gin palaces, publichouses will music licenses, with their vulgar buff coaery always open, yet places of worship were periodically closed for the week. It was the duty of the church to control and direct amuse- ments into pure and holy channels. Religion was never designed to make their pleasures less. No man was in his right mental condition to consider the difficult problem of recreation on Its highest ground until be had surrendered himself to God It was a delusion that theatre going, dancing, and card playing were the only form of worldlinees. Those Pharasoical people who tkought that they at any rate were free from worldliness because they never went to the theatre, danced, or played cards, made a mistake, because some of the most intensely wicked worldly Wesleyans he had ever known had never done any of those three things and had been boasting they were not as other men were. The preaches then gave a definition of genuine manliness, and closed by asking the young men to take a deeper inter- est in the moral and social welfare of tee town. Let them stand shoulder to shoulder and sweep the town of its intemperance, gambling, and impurity, for it seemed that the liquor trade had got its foot down on nearly every agency. Let them have generations of young men who would fear God and keep his commandments, and the future promsrity of Warrington was secured, and they would be emoncipated trim its toils, which bad blighted so many of their townsmen. In the unavoidable absence of Ex-Quarter-Master Sergeant Rkerts,the chair at the meeting of this society on Sunday was occupied by Mr. William Beswick. After referring to a recent newspaper correspondence on "WhV people don't go to char eh" he proceeded to speak on "Faith." Mr. Grorge J. B:awn delivered the address. He based his remarks on the text "And a wan shall be as an larding place from the wind, and a covert from the tempest ;as rivers of water in a dry place, as the shaaow of a great rock in a weary land" Isaiah 32c., 2v. After a reference ta the times in which Isaiah lived, the speaker said it was right and proper fir them to ask in how far were they in their lives trying to carry out the teaching of the text. There were hundreds who needed extricating from their evil surroundings,and this was a work in which they might engage. They often sang "Rescue the perishing, care for tas dying " but did they do so? He concluded by asking them to make their lives one of service and Christ would give them their reward. The soloi.t for the afterrion was Mr. William Parker. The service was also taken part in by Mr. T. Wallington (secretary), Mr. Hawthorn, and Mr. S. Row- lands.

OVER £l,OOO REALISED. On Saturday, the eoncludine day, the Warrington Parish Church Bazaar held in Parr Hall in aid at the church schools and missions was opened in the presence of a large company by the Mayor and Mayoress (Alder- man J. Rand Mrs. Wright). The RECTOR said that the total amount realised up to date was £920. They must not forget, however, that they needed another £l,OOO in the immediate future It was a very groat pleasure to do honour tc the Mayor and Maysiess on that imeortant occasion. He felt justly proud that the two first duties wh oh the Mayor nad inaugurated his new year of office were attending service at the Parish Church and opening ehe Parish Church Bazaar. (Applause.) Miss Nellie Dunn then presented the Mayoress with a t radsome bouquet , and the Mayor received from Master Tarn Crain a bound copy of the bazaar hand book. The MAY" in declaring the bazaar open, expressed the pleasure it atTorded hien to perform the duty. He thcareht if thanks were due to anyone they were due to the lady workers. For months past they had been work- ing early and late for the bazaar, and he had no douot the result would be very gratifying, to them. He did net know how sufficiently to thank their neighbours both in Cheshire and Lancashire for coming and helping them. Without ths;r valuable help they would never have had success. Before the end of the day they would be able, he thought, to say that they had realised all they ex- pected, and probably more besides. ME EXAMINER.

NOVEMBER 25th, 1893.

ONE of the most experienced The Law of. of grand juries, being asked Evidence. their opinion on the working of the new Law of Evidence, have declared that it tends to the conviction of the guilty—by which is meant that it nroduces a larger percentage of convictions than could be obtained by ordinary methods. Sir HARRY BODKIN POLAND, ill his appreciation of the Act in a letter to the " Times' —he was one of its extra-Parliamentary sponsors —has also been forced to admit that it has pro. duced a very large increase in perjury. This will continue until offenders find that their falsehoods will, if detected, afterwards be made the subject of a fresh indictment as a warning to the public. In New South Wales that has been done, a prisoner, who obtained his acquittal by perjury, being afterwards tried and sentenced for this de- parture from public morals. To a man whose life or a long period of liberty hi jeopardised, the tempta- tion which the law now puts in his way is well-nigh irresistible. The case of DR. WHIT., MARSH is a forcible example. With the shadow of the gallows hanging over him, he was led to go into the witness-box and elaborate a detailed story of his innocence in a matter about which few per. sons, except the minority of the first jury who tried him, could have any serious doubt. His forced statements are generally supposed to have strengthened the case for the prosecution, but everyone would have been glad if such a painful scene could have been avoided. There is no doubt cases will occur in which a prisoner's explanation of his position under the seleinnity of the oath will bring about his acquittal; but the fault of the Act is that it allows judges to comment on a prisoner's refusal to go into the witness-box, although of course, counsel may not. That power will be used or avoided by judges according to their personal views, and it is most desirable, in the interests of justice, that seine principle of uniformity should be adopted. That principle, in the opinion of many lawyers. should be not to take advantage of the Act, and so to force an unwilling man to swear against his conscience. His appearance in the boX should Lot be a necessity.

THE controversy of the past few The Bishops and months as to the duties and obli- Rituabsca.

_ gations of the Bishops and the clergy ought to bear useful fruit. It will be little less that disastrous to the interests of Protestant- ism and tho Church if, in the absence of remedial steps, Parliament should have to interpose with another enactment, or earnest men should come

to the inevitable conclusion that more drastie action is imperative. There have been some in- timations as to the action of, individual Bishop 3 who have never had any leanings towards what called A.nglo-Catholicism. There have been abundant indications of considerabl value with respect to the trend of public opini°n Whatever may be urged upon historical, theologi- cal, or 23 sthetic grounds, the overwhelming opinion of the country is Protestant. It is against imita" tions of Romish practices and the eccentricities. o

ct ,vidual clergymen.On the other hand,it does not to persecute and make martyrs, and throw , whole country into confusion when so many 6her questions are stirring. It would much rather Tae. Bishops undertook some united moderating an with the view of presenting the growth of 41:tees sea distinctly Roma' h in character. IThe Bishops are understood to have had a con- on the subject, and it is time we had some Nermation as to the conclusions at which they , -dyed, if they were able to agree to any common L of action. The hint is being given that L. ir decision was not to be made public. Surely ' . would be a mistake in principle as well as ~ 'lcy. If the public knew what practices were t,la be condemned as foreign to the Articles and 418 ages of the Church, and what tests were to be : to candidates for ordination,a much calmer rt would prevail. The aim of the con- ,reversy has been to elucidate these matters. Bishops cannot be ignorant of the , ition of public feeling. They cannot .118 indifferent to the peace and discipline of the Church. They cannot wholly ignore the -station which has been put on their and its duties—nor the rumours that are , circulated regarding the outcome of their cerence. Accordingly, it is rather playing with question if, after having attained to some agreement, they leave the public and the clergy in absolute ignorance about it. We know that Ititualism is a very vague term, and that there are diZerences of opinion as to what it means. But tlaere is no vagueness about Protestantism, and its ritualistic difference from Romaniana. If, therefore, the Bishops have come to some idea as to what practices they will not sanction, it is but aonsmon prudence on their part to make ,the details known. They have nothing to fear and averything to hope for from honesty and plain- dealing, and the public judguaent will go against them if they keep private the means they intend to take to meet the public demands.

One of the things no Englishman professes to ,13Zderstand about the citizens of the United States !E; the cheerfulness with which they submit to all kinds of libellous and morally unclean mat termn the newspapers, of well as to the public discussion of purely private concerns, which in England are sedulously guarded as family property. They were having a discussion in a well-known New York °Alb the other day concerning the relations be- tween journalism and the right of privacy. so far as can be ascertained, the large majority of the sPeakers agreed that the journalist had a right to Print any information he could obtain. Minute eporting of divorce cases, for instance, was in- eisted upon, as the information was likely to do good. Herein England exactly the opposite view le held, and has, indeed,,been proved correct. The Police say that the publication of train outrages by boys leads to fresh crimes. It is notorious that ' several cases of crime of an unmentionable and in- famous character took place after the condemna- tion of the prisoner in a celebrated trial some Years ago. In that case all the unsavoury details were 'appraised by editors of newspapers. In America they would be given in full, we are told, as moral lesson We are not in a position to s,ay whether such a lesson has valuable results in Araerica. Americans say that it has,and we must accept their word; ,but the conclusion seems to Point to a remarkable difference in Anglo- Ataerican temperament.

We should hardly have thought the Mayor desirous of provoking that comparison, which is proverbially odious, between himself and the Duke of Westminster, yet this is what he did by his unwise action on iVednesday in attending the Parish Church Bazaar in his robes of office, escorted by the Corporation mace and sword bearers in full regalia. His Grace—the descendant of noble ancestors and the possessor of many °Aliens—appeared before the public simply at- tired as an English gentleman, while His Worship, the Mayor of Warrington, was decked out in all ois insignia, and had in atter,dance the gorgeous bearers of the sword and mace. On the part of observers comparison was inevitable. But the ain point of the whole affair is that the may& had neither right nor title to insist on the attendance of the two public functionaries Who accompanied him. They are the servants solely of the Corporation, and the Mayor of the borough only has a claim on their services on such easions as a ceremony officially attended by the ‘',erporation. We are ready to believe that his worship was unaware of this fact, but he tioVertheless committed a serious breach of e.tirinette. Were these officials compelled to at- upon the Mayor whenever he chose to visit a gathering of this character, their office would run _t,itie risk of being deprived of its dignity and ren 'eared ridiculous.

at The bazaar has also brought into prominence XO fact that such functions are often attended by Tilerabers of the light fingered fraternity. At pre- bazaars we have heard of pockets having *an stealthily picked, but this is the first time a 44111301der at it Warrington bazaar i has had to la- _lgent the loss of her satchel containing ready cash Mounting to more than £l7. The work of track- tag the depredator is an exceedingly difficult one, ?hit it is to be hoped that the police may succeed bringing the individual to book. Councillor ohn Crosfield has again displayed his magnani- kity by handing to the hapless stallholder a 'elaeque for the amount stolen.

litt, Many people in Warrington will be pleased to arn that arrangements have been made for a tries of Saturday evening, concerts .in the Parr all, similar to those held last season. The first will tile, given next Saturday evening.lt was at one time "gought inadvisable to continue them, and we do ilot suppose that from a monetary point of view the ~ orporation will be the gainers by renewing them, .014 as wehave seen in the art exhibition for in- atance, the Corporation at times sink mere mone- tary considerations in the interests and for the cnitertainment of the townspeople. The concerts pre l'allsly given have been of an expensive nature,and ' iequires a full hall to enable the committee to re- "up themselves for the outlay. But it would boa " eat pity if the Parr Hall were to be allowed to °Main unused on Saturday evenings when it 'tight be put to such excellent use. Viewed hi . B light of a counter-attraction to the public- Ouse, the concerts even at a small loss, would be ' Ostifiable.

:cultists and fruit growers in some dis- ^ cts of England are complaining that the pro- ' 'ttion afforded to birds is tending to a multipli- :tion of the species which is prejudical to their .terests. The tits, *Pin, are playing havoc with the wall fruit, and 4,..4e blackbirds and thrashes are indiscriminate in k Loi' r free love of berries of all kinds. The remains of Arthur Wildams, lately of 31, Albert- street, Ruecorn, who was one of the victims of the Morpeth dock disaster, and a young man highlr re- spected amongsc all classes, were interred at Runcorn Cemetery on Sunday afternoon. The solemn obsequies were attended by some 400 persons, including repre- sentatives from Camden Sunday School and Chapel,Run- corn Sons of TallperanCO, Runcorn Liberal Club. and about 100 members of the Upper Mersey Watermen's and Porters' Association. The latter assembled at their headquarters, the Brunner Guild Hall, and walked to the deceased's late residence, where they joined the cortege. The burial service was conducted by the Rev. J.A.B. Malvern. At the graveside funeral hymns were sung by the large concourse assembled. The coffin was covered with beautiful floral tributes. Si. Peue's P.S.E. WIDNES. A RUNCORN MAN FOUND DROWN.

At the it-u-t:cwo-r-n-Pelice Court on Tuesday. Mr. J. 0. Noesley, coroner, held an incelest on the body of Themaes Henry Hardley who was found on a sand hank at Weeton Point on Sunday morning.—Mary Jane Hard- iest, widow of the deceased, said she mailed at 14. Stanley-street, Runcorn. The deceased was a labourer and was between 38 and 40 years of age. On Saturday, the 19th lust., he was not in good heelth and spirits. On Friday last he bad four fits. He bad never said any- thing about destroying himself.—James Wailing, water- man, deposed to having seen the body of the deeeesed on a sand bank opposite the Dalamere Dock at Weston Point, about five yards from the embankment. He gave information to the police.—P.C. Bancroft said from in- formation received he recovered the body of the de- ceased. He examined the body but found no marks of violence. Witness had since ascertained that the de- ceased was sebj et to fits.—The Coroner said there was no evidence to room how the deceased got into the water and he thoureht the only verdict the jury could return was one of found drowned. Toe jury agreed to this verdict. TRIBUTE TO TIII3I LATE MR. GANDY. On Sunday evening a memorial service was held at the United Methodist Free Church, Runcorn, with refer- ence to the late Mr. Edward Gandy. The Rev. S. Wright (pastor) said few men had a better claim to have their memory perpetuated than the subject of this brief memoir, Mr. Edward Gaudy. 'Their deceased friend was born in Runcorn on June 19th, 1843, so that his valuable life terminated in his 57th year, when his character was ripest and his powers for service were the most mature. As to any distinction of character' attelnment or service which he reached, he was the architect of his own for- tunes, and was not in the least indebted to the precari- ous accidents of wealth, position, em influence. His parents were among the earliest adherents to that church, and in the qualities and services of their son Edward the church received from them a heritage of iu- oalculable value. Their deceased brother was early sent to the Sunday school attached to that place, and it was always a pleasant reeollectien to him that, in his spare time, he assisted, with his own hands, in the erection of the building with which he watt to be long and so honourably associ- ated, and in connection with which his devoted labeure would be an imperishable mem ary. At 17 years of age Mr. Gandy connected himself with that church, and for 40 years he was devotedly attached to all its institu- tions and assisted in ite movements. Fr 30 years be was an active trustee of those premises, and for 27 yeare he was eecreeary to the trust. For 15 years Mr. Gandy rendered acceptable service as a local prsecher, and at his decease he was circuit secretary, an office which Ile had held for some years. Bet it wag in the Sabbath school that their departed friend found his most loved and most appreciated sphere of usefulness. For 27 yeare Mr. Gandy was teacher of the senior classes of the school, and for 24 years he was Sunday school superin- tendent. The last special effort in which he was chief actor in their behalf and which he carried to a success- ful Lane, was that of procuring for the school a new banner. THE EXAIMNER FRIDAY, NOVEMBER ,25, IS9B

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORaESPONDENT.] The one topic for the town is the Scho)l Board Election, and there is going to be quite a stiff fight for the nine seats. To aid a little spice to the pro- ceedings there is now an unexpected party in the contest, viz; Ir. W. Collier whose chances of suc- cess are many, as he is really the only independent candidate of the lot, and those who admire inde- pendence and freedom have in him a man upon whom they can rely.

It will be noticed that Mr. W. J. Cunningham, takes the place of Mr. Joseph Pritchard in the ranks of the" Ratepayers " Candidate' Mr. Prit- chard having, I understand throug a illness, been compelled to retire in his favour.

That the fight is not to ba a p3litical one is very evident, for there are nearly all shades of political opinion joined together under the assumed titles of the two contesting parties. Still, I think that the ratepayers will best serve themselves by making a really conscientious selection of 'those men who have their absolute confidence.

It might be well to state that in this election all the parties concerned seemingly want to maka it appear that there is to be nlttaestion of religion, or denomination at issue, but the fact remains that the five progressive candidates are Nonconformists, and the four ratepayers' candidates are Churchmen, 80 that it is perfectly clear what the election will finally revolve into.

At the County Court on Tuesday Judge Wynne Foulkes performed a record sitting for Runcorn in the hearing of a case, which extended for over 5. hours, and Which was adjourned to another Court.

FOOTBALL. The Cheshire v. Cumboland match at Runcorn goes to show that County football is losing a great deal of its former lustre. Indeed, the bast part of it was that which the " gate" did not see—the after proceedings.

Cheshire were minus S.Hough`on, who was inca- pacitated the other week in the match at Salford. But it is pleasing to record thu the time-honoured veteran is getting on as well as can be expected. It is to be hoped that his recovery will be complete and rapid, a 3 his Services are really indispensable to Runcorn just at this critical stage. •

Cumberland's biggest draw-back was perhaps the fact that they were m nus their "County jerseys " and as we all know a "County jersey"is tan immense factor in a struggle of this kind. These little up- sets have to be endured occasionally, but county matches are such rare occurrences that a little better provision ought to ba made to ensure against them.

RUNCORN PHILEATUIONIC SOCIETY'S

CONCERT.

The concert promoted by the Runcorn Philhar- monic Society attracted a select and fashionable audience to the Public Hall, Runcorn, on Wednes- day evening, when it was submitted with unquali- fied success. The artistes for the occasion were: Miss Alice A. Simons (soprano) ; Mr, Charles Saun- • dors (tenor) ; with the Runcorn Orchestral Society and the Philharmonic Society, (the latter:band and chorus numbering close upon 120). The conductor was Hr. C. T. Reynolds, Hiss Doc (Oxon) ; band leader, Mr. M. Kruger; and the honorary accom• panist of the choir was Miss Barbara Johnson. The programme provided was a capital one, and was rendered most capably throughout. Commencing with Sir A, Sullivan's dramatic cantata' On Shore and Sea," all the parts of which are, musical masterpieces, the choir gave a good account of itself in all the choruses. The soloists— Miss Alice A Simons (LaSposina), and Mr Charles Saunders (II Marinajo)—were 'good. Miss Simons excelled in the recitative " From spring time, etc," whilst Mr. Sanders was in perfect voice all through, but in the rendering of the duett " Here on thy heart" both were warmly encored for harmonious 'vocalisation. The opening piece of the 2nd part, "Banana," by the orchestra was well received. The part song "The lonely vale of streams" (Calleot), was short, sweet, and crisp, and displayed the abilities of the choir to perfection. This item was the choir's best performance. Miss Simons fellowecl with a song" Roberto oh I tu cbe adoro " (Meyer - beer), and treated the audience to a masterly inter- preta ion of the piece. It was captivating, and Miss Simons can lay claim to a really fine' powerful, voice capable of much expression. As encore she sang a lullaby pleasing and pretty. Teen came the success of the evening, "Bianca," (Tito Mattei), by Mr Charles Saunders. It was splendidly sung, the expression and foaling displayed by the artiste in a voice of remarkable compass drawing forth the spontaneous enclmiums of the audience. As encore he gave "Torn Bawling," and seldom have we listen4d to such a "Tom Bowling; " this called forth a vlciferous recall, and Mr Saunders responded with "Sweet tales of Araby.' The choir thea gave the" Soldier's Chorus" (from Gounod'a "Faust "), which they rendered in vigorous style. The instrumental portion of the c3ncert received musicianly treatment at the hands of the orchestra, who added to the good opinions formed of them by the townspeople. Dr. Reynolds was the accom- panist, and he acquitted himself worthily. The whole of the arrangements for the concert were in the hands of the committee and secretary, who deserve high credit for briogiug their labours to so successful an issue.

MEETING AT THE MARINERS' CHURCH. 01 Wednesday night there was a fur tber meeting of the ratepayers' candidates held at the Mariner; Church, Mr. W. Fisher presiding over a good attendance. Mr. Liaison delivered an address in which he pointed out that the Church party were net responsible for the elec. tisn. They had no desire to disturb the present arrange- ments, hut as their opponents claimed a right to have five candidates they were forced to fight. The statement that they were sectarian candidates was unreliable and misleading. The fact that they had been in a majority on the School Board for sore' years laid them open to be blamed for any mistakes that the Board had male ; but

it also entitled them to whatever credit was due. He pointed out the value of the Voluntary Schools of the town,showing that they were a saving to them of is 2d in the pound on the rates. Sy figures ha illustrated how the School Board had incre seed their expenses from the inception of the Board in 1879 until the present time. For the first ten years of the existence of the Board the average payment of the School Board from the poor rate was £351, and in the next ten years it was £895 per year, and for the last four years the payment ta them was £1962 10s. That showed in a very striking manner the value of the Voluntary Schools to the ratepayers. By the end of March next year thPSchool Board would have received fr sm the rates £19,460, while the School Board debt was £8,409. Addresses were also delivered by the other candidates Mr. W. J. Cunningham, Mr. John Littler, and the Rev. Canon Wood, all of whom were heartily received. A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the proceedings. On Friday and Saturday, the Runcorn, Widnes and District Bird Breeders' Association held their annual ex- hibitien of canaries, and British birds, in the auction room Central Hotel. Widnes. RUNCORN COUNTY COURT.

TUESDAY.- Before his Honour Juige Wynne.Roultres.

THE LIST comprised 63 ordinary summones, 19 judgment summonses, 10 defana summonses, and 2 adjourned actiods. CILLIEHON BETWEEN A TRAP AND A MILE.FLOAT. CLAIM FOR DAMAGES.—George Acton, farmer Pool Farm, Heath' road, Runcorn brought an action against Robert Shepherd, farmer Parkside Farm, Aston. to recover 211 9s. 6d. for damage incurred by the plaintiff through a collision which took place between his trap and a milk cart belonging to the defendant, owing it was alleged, to the negligent driving of the defendant's servant. Greer. barrister (instructed by Mr J. Burton) appeared for the plat ,tiff and Mr. Jordan, , barrister. (instructed by Mr. H. N. Linaker) represented the defendant. In opening the case Mr. Greer stated that at the time of the accident the plaintlf's trap was being driven by Walter Gerrard, and the other occup nte of the trap were Mrs. Acton. mother of the plaintiff, two young sons of Mr. Gerrard and another boy named Leonard Pye. The accident occurred on the 23rd October about 6p m. Acton-lane. between Lowe's-lane and Marsden'a Corner. The road was about 16 feet wide with a ditch on one side. Gerrard had driven to Dutton and was returning driving in the direction of Frodsham, whoa he SAW coming in the opposite direetlon, and occupying about the middle of the road, a milk float, belonging to the defendant. There were two people in it apparently engaged in conversation and the reins were down loosely in front, neither moo having hold of them. Gerrard celled out "Rh up" but no attention was-paid to him and he called a second time and the man took up the reins and endeavoured to get past. Seeing the position of affairs Gerrard puled up on the grass on the side of the road as near the ditch as he dare Just as the float reached the trap the man took up a stick and struck the horse,causing it to rear and plunge with the result that the fl ,at collided with the trap and the pony made a bound for- ward, being startled. Both ihafts of the trap were smashed, the pony ran away and all the occupants except one boy were thrown out into the road. The trap and harness were badly broken. The trap was dragged six yards by the float. Gerrard picked Mrs. Aeton up, she was injured and he placed her against the cep while be ran and captured the pony. When Gerrard returned to the place, Shepherd came up who offered to pay for the repair of one shaft. He further stated that his men had informed him that the trap was driven by an old man uncompetent to drive it. This statement wis corroborated by Walter Gerrard, commission agent. 33, Waterloo-road, Runcorn. Mrs. Ashton. who was confined to b d and her evidence taken on commission, and a number of other witnesses.—For the defence Mr, Jordan reserve i his address to the jury and called Charles Percival, the driver of the milk fbat, wh) deposed to seeing the trap coming towards him in the middle of the road. A person sitting In frout was holding up an umbrella. The trap was in the middle of the road. The road WAS not a wide one but there was plenty of rooma for two vehicles to pass. Witness shouted to the trao "Eli up I Look out! Where are you going 2" The trap was then 20 or 30 yards away. The trap kept on corning along. Wit- ners was going at a very slow pane, and when he saw the trap was not gettinz out of the way he drew the float ten or twelve inches on the parapet ; he emir' not take it on any further without going into the hechre. The trap drove straight into him. The lan hes of the wheels jammed together, the pony made two plunges, the shafts broke and off went the pony. Witness was chucked on the front board by the violence of the collision, but he regained his control of his horse and drew up in three yards. He denied ever striking the animal with a whip or a stick ;he had nothing of the sort in the float. He was not drunk. No beer was allowed on the farm at all.—Crosa-examined n Witness said be was not talking to the by. nor had he let the reins go. He had the horse perfectly in hand all the time. When he shouted he could be heard half a mile away. Gerrard did not seera to hear him, and he did not think he could isPe him at all because of the umbrella. At the time of the accident Gerrard' pony was trotting. He never heard Gerrard shout, witness's horse was going at a jog trot.—Tnemas Bartington, the other occupant of the float, and other witnesses were called, but thecase had not concluded when the Court rose. It will be concluded at the next Court, on December 15th. A jury was empannelled to try the case.

FREE TRIAL OF SOMETHING THAT WILL DO.

You would be perfectly astonished if you were made aware of the many thousands of pounds absolutely thrown away from year to year upon so-called curatives that are foisted upon a public only too willing to believe the specious argumems laid before them.

Even the hard earned shillings of the very poor are wasted in this way ; in fact, it is to the ignorant anxious to rid themselves of the various ailments which handicap them in the race for life, that such arguments are too often addressed. Now strength and muscular activity, rosy cheeks, plumpness, and health can be obtained without medicine. _ _

The replenishing of the system from the wasting of tissues which is going on every day can only be accomplished by the proper assimilation of food. It cannot be done with medicine. It can, however, be accomplished with a perfect, flesh-forming, palatable, and agreeable Food Beverage. EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

P.S.A. At the usual weekly gathering of the P.S,A., on Sunday afternoon there was a fairly numerous attendance. Mr. James Smith presided, end an excellent address was given by Mr. G. Dring, missionary. Recitations were given by Miss M. Ord, and the vocal selections were rendered by Mr. F. P. Clare. ORGAN RECITAL AT EAELESTOWN. On Sunday afternoon, Mr. H. W. Radford, organ- ist at St. John's Cnursh, gave a faresvAll organ recital, previous to his resignation of the position. The proceeds were toward., clearing off the balance remaining on the organ repair fund. Peplow; organ solo, "Barcarolle F";in vocal solo." The rleavenly Song," Miss A. Clarke ; violin solo, "Romance in F," Mr. replow ; organ solo, "Can'il en e Pastorale''; organ solo, "Grand Fugue in E flat" and "Coronation March." PRESEVATION TO AN ORGANIST. Mr. IT. W. Radford, organist at St. John's Church, Fatlestown, having accepted nno'her position in a similar caoacity, was on Friday evening the recipient of a purse of gold, as e fidence of the appreciation in which his services were held by the congregation and clergy. A social evening was held in the dist-ict schoolroom, about 100 persons being present. An excellent t-a was partaken of, and at the close of the meal the presentation was made by the vicar (Rev. T. R. Matthews), who ENO presen- ted Mrs. Radfo-d with a gold bracelet. Tile Rev F. Sutcliffe also spoke eulogistically of Mr. and Mrs. Radford, and Mr. Findlay added his quota. Mr. Radford thanked them for their. kindness. During the evening, songs were given by Miss A. Clarke, and violin selen'ions by Mr. J. Lewis. Mr. Dukes presided at t`,e niano. PRESENTATION TO A CHORISTER. There was a very pleasant gathering at the Mis- sion Hall, rarlestown, on Tuesday evening of members of the choir of St. John's Church, and teachers of the S inday school, for the purpose of showing Mr. W. lii„zerion. a former member of the choir, and a teacher in the Sunlay school, in practi- cal form their esteem and appreciation of his ser- vices during his connection with the church,he hay- ing now left the neighbourhood.
